{
  "schema_version": "ATIF-v1.7",
  "session_id": "luxuriant-pheasant",
  "agent": {
    "name": "devin",
    "version": "3000.10.21",
    "model_name": "SWE-2 Max",
    "tool_definitions": [
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "ask_user_question",
          "description": "Present multiple-choice questions to the user and collect their answers.\n\nUse this tool when you need the user to make a decision between several options, such as choosing\nan implementation approach, selecting a library, or confirming a design choice.\n\nKey constraints:\n- 1-4 questions per call\n- 2-4 options per question (an \"Other\" free-text option is always added automatically)\n- Keep headers short (e.g. \"Auth method\", \"Library\"); headers over 16 characters are truncated\n- Option labels should be 1-5 words\n\nThe user can:\n- Select from the predefined options\n- Type a custom answer via the \"Other\" option\n- Skip or decline the question to ask for clarification\n\nIf the user submits answers, you will receive a key-value mapping of question text to their selections.\nQuestions the user skipped are marked with \"skipped\": true — treat a skipped question as the user\ndeclining to choose, not as an error, and proceed with your best judgment.\nIf the user chooses to chat instead, you will receive a rejection with any partial answers they provided.",
          "parameters": {
            "additionalProperties": false,
            "required": [
              "questions"
            ],
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
              "questions": {
                "description": "Array of 1-4 question objects to present to the user.",
                "type": "array",
                "items": {
                  "type": "object",
                  "properties": {
                    "question": {
                      "description": "The full question text to display to the user.",
                      "type": "string"
                    },
                    "header": {
                      "description": "Short label displayed as a chip/tag, e.g. \"Auth method\", \"Library\".\nLabels longer than 16 characters are truncated with an ellipsis (\"…\") for display.",
                      "type": "string"
                    },
                    "options": {
                      "description": "The choices presented to the user (2-4 options). An \"Other\" free-text option is always added automatically.",
                      "type": "array",
                      "items": {
                        "type": "object",
                        "properties": {
                          "label": {
                            "description": "Display text the user sees (1-5 words).",
                            "type": "string"
                          },
                          "description": {
                            "description": "Explanation of what this option means or its trade-offs.",
                            "type": "string"
                          }
                        },
                        "required": [
                          "label",
                          "description"
                        ],
                        "additionalProperties": false
                      }
                    },
                    "multi_select": {
                      "description": "If true, the user can select multiple options; if false, single-select only.",
                      "type": "boolean",
                      "default": false
                    }
                  },
                  "required": [
                    "question",
                    "header",
                    "options"
                  ],
                  "additionalProperties": false
                }
              },
              "answers": {
                "description": "User's answers, keyed by question text. Populated by the UI when the user responds.\nDo not set this yourself; it will be filled in automatically.",
                "type": "object",
                "additionalProperties": {
                  "description": "Represents a single answer from the user for one question.",
                  "type": "object",
                  "properties": {
                    "selected": {
                      "description": "The selected option label(s). For single-select, this will have one element.\nFor multi-select, it may have multiple. If \"Other\" was chosen, it will contain \"Other\".",
                      "type": "array",
                      "items": {
                        "type": "string"
                      }
                    },
                    "custom_text": {
                      "description": "Custom text provided by the user. Set when the user selects \"Other\".",
                      "type": "string"
                    }
                  },
                  "required": [
                    "selected"
                  ],
                  "additionalProperties": false
                }
              }
            }
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "browser_preview",
          "description": "Spin up a browser preview for a running web server.\n\nThis lets the USER interact with the web server as normal, and lets them send\nyou what they see: selecting a DOM element (with its React component and source\nlocation, when resolvable) or the browser console output. Those captures go into\nthe USER's message box for them to send — you will not receive them unless they\ndo, so ask them to share what they see rather than waiting.\n\nUsage notes:\n  - The server must already be running; start it with a background command\n    first (`timeout: 0`) if it isn't.\n  - Returns immediately with a preview ID and URL. The preview stays up for the\n    rest of the session, so the USER can keep using it after you stop.\n  - An attempt is made to open the URL in the USER's browser; the URL is always\n    printed in case that fails (e.g. over SSH or in a container).\n  - Calling this again for the same URL returns the existing preview.\n  - Use `close_browser_preview` when the preview is no longer needed, e.g.\n    before shutting down the server it points at.\n",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "url",
              "name"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "url": {
                "description": "The URL of the target web server to provide a browser preview for. This\nshould contain the scheme (e.g. http:// or https://), domain (e.g.\nlocalhost or 127.0.0.1), and port (e.g. :8080) but no path.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "name": {
                "description": "A short 3-5 word name for the target web server. Should be title-cased\ne.g. 'Personal Website'. Format as a simple string, not as markdown; and\nplease output the title directly, do not prefix it with 'Title:' or\nanything similar.",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "close_browser_preview",
          "description": "Closes a running browser preview by its ID.\n\n- Takes a preview_id parameter, as returned by `browser_preview`\n- The preview URL stops working; the web server it points at is untouched\n- Previews are closed automatically when the session ends, so this is only\n  needed to close one early\n",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "preview_id"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "preview_id": {
                "description": "The ID of the browser preview to close.",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "edit",
          "description": "Performs exact string replacements in files.\n\nUsage:\n- You must use your `read` tool at least once in the conversation before editing. This tool will error if you attempt an edit without reading the file.\n- When editing text from read tool output, ensure you preserve the exact indentation (tabs/spaces) as it appears AFTER the line number prefix. The line number prefix format is: spaces + line number + tab. Everything after that tab is the actual file content to match. Never include any part of the line number prefix in the old_string or new_string.\n- ALWAYS prefer editing existing files to creating new ones.\n- Only use emojis if the user explicitly requests it. Avoid adding emojis to files unless asked.\n- The edit will FAIL if `old_string` is not unique in the file. Either provide a larger string with more surrounding context to make it unique or use `replace_all` to change every instance of `old_string`.\n- Use `replace_all` for replacing and renaming strings across the file. This parameter is useful if you want to rename a variable for instance.",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "file_path",
              "old_string",
              "new_string"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "file_path": {
                "description": "The absolute path to the file to modify",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "old_string": {
                "description": "The text to replace. Always provide `old_string` before `new_string` so that streaming displays can show the diff progressively.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "new_string": {
                "description": "The text to replace it with (must be different from `old_string`)",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "replace_all": {
                "description": "Replace all occurrences of `old_string` (default false)",
                "type": "boolean",
                "default": false
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "exec",
          "description": "Executes a given shell command in a persistent shell session and waits for output with optional timeout, ensuring proper handling and security measures.\n\nCommands run under bash by default on every platform (Git Bash on Windows; PowerShell only if Git Bash is missing) unless you set `shell_flavor`. Write the command in that shell's syntax; never wrap it in another shell (`bash -lc \"...\"`, `powershell -Command \"...\"`), which parses your quoting and `$` expansions twice. To use a different shell, set `shell_flavor` (e.g. `shell_flavor: \"powershell\"` for Windows-native cmdlets).\n\nIMPORTANT: This tool is for terminal operations like git, npm, docker, etc. DO NOT use it for file operations (reading, writing, editing, searching, finding files) - use the specialized tools for this instead.\n\nBefore executing the command, please follow these steps:\n\n1. Directory Verification:\n   - If the command will create new directories or files, first use `ls` to verify the parent directory exists and is the correct location\n   - For example, before running \"mkdir foo/bar\", first use `ls foo` to check that \"foo\" exists and is the intended parent directory\n\n2. Command Execution:\n   - Always quote file paths that contain spaces with double quotes (e.g., cd \"path with spaces/file.txt\")\n   - Examples of proper quoting:\n     - cd \"/Users/name/My Documents\" (correct)\n     - cd /Users/name/My Documents (incorrect - will fail)\n     - python \"/path/with spaces/script.py\" (correct)\n     - python /path/with spaces/script.py (incorrect - will fail)\n   - After ensuring proper quoting, execute the command.\n   - Capture the output of the command.\n\nUsage notes:\n  - If the output is too long, it will be truncated before being returned to you.\n  - Commands run in a persistent shell session for each shell_id, preserving state between calls. The working directory persists between commands.\n  - One-shot commands (no `shell_id`) do NOT share state with each other: the working directory does NOT persist between calls. Use the optional `workdir` parameter (an absolute path to an existing directory) to set the working directory for a command, instead of a leading `cd` that the next call would forget. With a `shell_id`, `workdir` changes that session's directory before running the command.\n  - Prefer one command per call: to do several things, make several calls in the same message instead of separating commands with `;` or a newline.\n  - Chain with `&&` when a command must not run unless the previous one succeeded (PowerShell: `cmd1; if ($?) { cmd2 }`).\n  - Use absolute paths in your commands instead of `cd` if possible. You may use `cd` if the User explicitly requests it.\n  - Passwords cannot be entered into this tool, so `sudo` fails when it needs one. Do NOT retry with a TTY or ask the user to type a password — run without sudo or ask the user to run the command themselves.\n",
          "parameters": {
            "additionalProperties": false,
            "required": [
              "command"
            ],
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
              "command": {
                "description": "The command to execute in the current shell session.\n\nExamples:\n- Bash: \"echo 'Hello World'\", \"ls -la\", \"git status\"\n- PowerShell: \"Write-Output 'Hello World'\", \"Get-ChildItem\"\n\nNote: Do NOT include the shell executable (like \"bash -c\" or \"powershell -Command\") in the command.\nThe command should be the raw shell command as you would type it in the active shell.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "shell_id": {
                "description": "Optional shell ID to reuse an existing interactive session. Use shell IDs to maintain multiple shell sessions. When shell ID is provided, writes to that session's stdin. When no shell ID is provided, a new session is created.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "workdir": {
                "description": "Optional absolute path to use as the working directory for this command (does not persist between one-shot calls; with a `shell_id` it cds the session).",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "timeout": {
                "description": "Optional timeout in milliseconds (default: background after 10s idle or 30s total).\nWhen it elapses, a still-running command is backgrounded and its shell_id returned for `get_output`/`kill_shell`. Set to `0` to background immediately.",
                "type": "integer"
              },
              "tty": {
                "description": "Allocate a PTY-backed interactive session. Default `false`.\n`false` (default): one-shot non-interactive command run through pipes; good for most use cases.\n`true`: persistent interactive session — for TUI programs (vim, tmux, top, REPLs) or state that must persist across calls.",
                "type": "boolean",
                "default": false
              },
              "shell_flavor": {
                "description": "Which shell runs the command: `bash`, `zsh`, `fish` or `powershell`.\nDefaults to bash on every platform (Git Bash on Windows; PowerShell only\non a Windows machine without Git Bash). Pick a shell here instead of\nnesting one shell inside another: the command is handed to it verbatim,\nso `shell_flavor: \"powershell\"` runs plain PowerShell syntax with no\nbash parse of your quoting and `$` expansions in between. Only valid\nwhen starting a new shell: a `shell_id` that already exists keeps the\nshell it was started with.",
                "type": "string",
                "enum": [
                  "bash",
                  "zsh",
                  "fish",
                  "powershell"
                ]
              },
              "env": {
                "description": "Environment variables for this command's process tree, e.g.\n`{\"API_KEY\": \"abc123\", \"NODE_ENV\": \"development\"}`. These take effect\nonly when a new shell is started, so `env` cannot be combined with\n`shell_id` — omit `shell_id` (the call is rejected otherwise) so the\nvariables are set from the shell's first process onward. They are\nvisible to this command and its child processes only, not to other\nshells or later commands.",
                "type": "object",
                "additionalProperties": {
                  "type": "string"
                },
                "properties": {}
              }
            }
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "find_file_by_name",
          "description": "Fast file name/path pattern matching tool that works with any codebase size using glob patterns. Supports brace expansion (e.g., `**/*.{ts,tsx,js,jsx}`). Matches against file paths, not file contents. Do NOT use this tool for searching for things like function names, variable names, etc. which should be searched against file contents (e.g. using grep).",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "pattern"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "pattern": {
                "description": "The glob pattern to match files against.\nExamples:\n- `*.py` - matches all Python files in the current directory only (not subdirectories)\n- `**/*.js` - matches all JavaScript files in the current directory and all subdirectories recursively\n- `src/**/*.ts` - matches all TypeScript files within the `src` folder and its subdirectories\n- `test_*.py` - matches Python files starting with `test_` in the current directory (e.g., `test_utils.py`)\n- `**/*.{ts,tsx}` - matches all TypeScript files (both .ts and .tsx) recursively using brace expansion",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "path": {
                "description": "The directory to search in (defaults to current working directory)",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "get_output",
          "description": "Reads the output from a background shell process.\n\nUsage notes:\n- The shell_id argument is required (use the ID returned by the exec tool)\n- You can specify an optional timeout in milliseconds (up to 280000ms). Timeouts below 10000ms (10s) are raised to 10s; the default is 10s.\n- IMPORTANT: timeout is the MAX wait — this returns immediately when the process exits. If you want to wait for a finite command to finish, set a long timeout and do not repeatedly poll. For a process that is expected to keep running (dev servers, watchers, daemons), a long timeout always waits the full duration — use a short timeout (10-30s) to read its startup output, repeating until the ready signal appears, or verify readiness directly (e.g. curl the port).\n- The process continues running after reading output\n- This will return the output of the most recently executed command in the shell if there is no current command running command\n",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "shell_id"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "shell_id": {
                "description": "The ID of the shell to read output from.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "timeout": {
                "description": "Optional timeout in milliseconds.",
                "type": "integer"
              },
              "incremental": {
                "description": "If true (default), returns the changes since your last read. For interactive shells whose\nscreen redraws in place (progress bars, block redraws), this is a unified diff so in-place\nline changes are shown as `-`old / `+`new; for plain append-only output it is the new lines.\nIf false, returns the full output buffer.",
                "type": "boolean"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "grep",
          "description": "A powerful search tool built on ripgrep.\n\nUsage:\n- Always prefer this tool over invoking `grep` or `rg` as a shell command. It has been optimized for correct permissions and access.\n- The pattern is treated as a regular expression using Rust regex syntax (like `rg` defaults): look-ahead/look-behind (e.g. `(?!...)`, `(?<=...)`) and backreferences are NOT supported. Rewrite without them, e.g. use `foo\\([^)]` instead of `foo\\((?!\\))`.\n- Filter files with the glob_pattern parameter in glob format (e.g., \"*.rs\", \"src/**/*.py\").\n- Use output_mode to control results: 'content' for matching lines, 'files_with_matches' for file paths only, 'count' for match counts. Set case_insensitive for case-insensitive matching and context_lines to show lines around each match.\n- If the result is truncated, narrow your search with a more specific pattern or more filters.\n- Files larger than 4 MB are skipped.",
          "parameters": {
            "additionalProperties": false,
            "required": [
              "pattern"
            ],
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
              "pattern": {
                "description": "The regular expression pattern to search for, passed to ripgrep under the hood",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "path": {
                "description": "The directory or file to search in. Defaults to current directory.",
                "type": "string",
                "default": "."
              },
              "glob_pattern": {
                "description": "Glob pattern to filter files (e.g., '*.rs' searches .rs files in current dir but not subdirs, 'src/**/*.py' searches .py files in src/ and its subdirs). Defaults to searching all files.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "output_mode": {
                "type": "string",
                "enum": [
                  "content",
                  "files_with_matches",
                  "count"
                ],
                "description": "How to format the output.",
                "default": "content"
              },
              "case_insensitive": {
                "description": "Perform case-insensitive search. Defaults to false.",
                "type": "boolean",
                "default": false
              },
              "max_results": {
                "description": "Maximum number of matches to return. Defaults to 100, max 20,000. Only applicable for \"content\" output mode.",
                "type": "integer"
              },
              "context_lines": {
                "description": "Number of lines to show before and after each match (context). Defaults to 0.",
                "type": "integer",
                "default": 0
              }
            }
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "kill_shell",
          "description": "Kills a running background shell by its ID.\n\n- Takes a shell_id parameter identifying the shell to kill\n- Returns a success or failure status\n- Use this tool when you need to terminate a long-running shell\n",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "shell_id"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "shell_id": {
                "description": "The ID of the shell to kill.",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "mcp_call_tool",
          "description": "Execute a tool on an MCP server. Use this to interact with external services like Linear, GitHub, Slack, databases, and more. The tool result will be returned as structured data.",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "server_name",
              "tool_name"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "server_name": {
                "description": "Name of the MCP server to use (e.g., \"linear\", \"github\").",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "tool_name": {
                "description": "Name of the tool to execute.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "arguments": {
                "description": "Input arguments for the tool as a JSON object.",
                "type": "object",
                "additionalProperties": true,
                "default": {}
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "mcp_list_servers",
          "description": "Lists all MCP servers you have access to. Use this first if the user is asking about any third party integrations (e.g. Slack, Linear, etc).",
          "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {},
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "mcp_list_tools",
          "description": "List available tools and resources from MCP servers. Use this to discover what capabilities are available before calling mcp_call_tool.",
          "parameters": {
            "properties": {
              "server_name": {
                "description": "Name of the MCP server to list tools from (e.g., \"linear\", \"github\"). If not provided, lists tools from all configured servers.",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "mcp_read_resource",
          "description": "Read a resource from an MCP server. Resources can be files, database records, API responses, or any data exposed by the MCP server. Returns the resource content as text or binary data.",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "server_name",
              "resource_uri"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "server_name": {
                "description": "Name of the MCP server (e.g., \"linear\", \"github\").",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "resource_uri": {
                "description": "Resource URI to read (e.g., \"<file:///path/to/file>\", \"<linear://issue/123>\").",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "notebook_edit",
          "description": "Edit a cell in a Jupyter notebook file (.ipynb) - replace, insert, or delete cells",
          "parameters": {
            "additionalProperties": false,
            "required": [
              "notebook_path",
              "cell_number",
              "new_source"
            ],
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
              "notebook_path": {
                "description": "The absolute path to the Jupyter notebook file (.ipynb) to edit.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "cell_number": {
                "description": "The 0-based index of the cell to edit.",
                "type": "integer"
              },
              "new_source": {
                "description": "The new source content for the cell.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "cell_type": {
                "description": "The type of the cell. If not specified, keeps the current type (for replace) or defaults to code (for insert).",
                "type": "string",
                "enum": [
                  "code",
                  "markdown"
                ]
              },
              "edit_mode": {
                "default": "replace",
                "description": "The edit operation to perform. Defaults to replace.",
                "type": "string",
                "enum": [
                  "replace",
                  "insert",
                  "delete"
                ]
              }
            }
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "notebook_read",
          "description": "Read a Jupyter notebook file (.ipynb) and extract all cells with their outputs",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "notebook_path"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "notebook_path": {
                "description": "The absolute path to the Jupyter notebook file to read (must be absolute, not relative)",
                "type": "string"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "read",
          "description": "Reads a file from the filesystem. The file_path parameter must be an absolute path, not a relative path. By default, it reads up to 20000 characters starting from the beginning of the file. You can optionally specify a line offset and limit (especially handy for long files), but it's recommended to read the whole file by not providing these parameters. Any lines longer than 2000 characters will be truncated.",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "file_path"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "file_path": {
                "description": "The absolute path to the file to read.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "offset": {
                "description": "Optional line number to start reading from (1-based).",
                "type": "integer"
              },
              "limit": {
                "description": "Optional number of lines to read.",
                "type": "integer"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false
          }
        }
      },
      {
        "type": "function",
        "function": {
          "name": "read_subagent",
          "description": "Reads the response from a background subagent, using the agent_id you got from the run_subagent tool or a <subagent_completion_notification> message. You use block=true to wait for completion. If you have other work to do, you can do that (you'll be notified whenever a subagent completes). This tool will never interrupt a running subagent.",
          "parameters": {
            "required": [
              "agent_id"
            ],
            "properties": {
              "agent_id": {
                "description": "The ID of the background subagent to read output from.",
                "type": "string"
              },
              "block": {
                "description": "If true, block until the subagent finishes or the timeout expires.",
                "type": "boolean",
                "default": false
              },
              "timeout": {
                "description": "Maximum number of seconds to wait when blocking. Defaults to 30, capped at 600, unless the tool description documents different limits for a specific subagent type.",
                "type": "integer"
              }
            },
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false

          "name": "run_subagent",
          "description": "Launch an independent subagent to handle a task autonomously.\n\nSubagents (also referred to as just \"agents\") handle self-contained, multi-step tasks, such as broad or uncertain searches. When you do run several at once, launch them in parallel rather than one after the other, and keep their work disjoint so they don't interfere with each other (e.g. no two subagents writing the same files).\n\nSkip subagents when simpler tools suffice:\n- If you already know the file path, read it directly\n- Use your grep and glob tools rather than subagents when you think they will meet your needs quickly without many attempts\n- Use your edit or read tools directly if only a few files are involved\n\n- Don't use a subagent to do singular tasks like running a command\n\nWriting effective prompts:\n- Subagents are stateless; they cannot ask clarifying questions, and they can't see any of your context. Front-load all context they might neeed: relevant file paths, function/class names, what you already know, and exactly what you need back.\n- State whether the subagent should make changes or only investigate. It has no visibility into the user's original request. When asking open-ended questions, explicitly state how thorough of an answer you want.\nUsage notes:\n- The user never sees subagent output directly, so you'll need to distill subagents' answers into your own response if you want to .\n- You can generally trust subagent results. Avoid re-doing their work unless something looks wrong.\n\n- Set is_background=true to run a background subagent without blocking. You will be notified automatically when it finishes with a <subagent_completion_notification> message. You can check on the status of a background subagent using your read_subagent tool. To wait for a subagent to complete to see its response, call read_subagent with block=true. Background agents are nice for parallelism and work that might take unexpectedly long, in which case you'd want to do other work while waiting.\n        - Set is_background=false to run a foreground subagent when you need the answer before continuing. You can't do any other work while a foreground subagent is running, and at most one foreground agent can run at once. Despite these restrictions, foreground users have a slightly nicer UX and are less frequently blocked by permission issues. So use is_background=false when you don't need paralellism and would have otherwise spawned a background agent then immediately called read_subagent with block=true.\n        - If you want to run both background subagents and a foreground subagent, run the background subagents first and then run the foreground subagent. Don't try to launch multiple foreground subagents in parallel; instead use background subagents or run the foreground subagents sequentially so you can see each subagent's output before launching the next one.",

          "name": "todo_write",
          "description": "Use this tool to create and manage a structured task list for your current session. It tracks your progress and gives the user visibility into what you are working on.\n\n## When to use\nUse this tool for multi-step tasks (3 or more distinct steps), non-trivial tasks that require planning, or when the user provides multiple tasks or explicitly asks for a todo list. Skip it for trivial, single-step, or purely conversational/informational requests — just do the task directly.\n\n## Task states and management\n- Each task is pending, in_progress, or completed\n- Keep exactly ONE task in_progress at a time; mark it in_progress when starting work on it\n- Mark a task completed IMMEDIATELY after finishing it\n- Only mark a task completed when it is fully done — if you hit errors or blockers, keep it in_progress and add a new task describing what must be resolved\n- Add follow-up tasks discovered during work; remove tasks that are no longer relevant\n- Create specific, actionable items and break complex work into smaller steps\n\n## Example\nFor \"Add a dark mode toggle to settings, then run tests and build\":\n1. Add dark mode toggle component to settings page (in_progress)\n2. Wire up dark mode state management\n3. Update existing components to support theme switching\n4. Run tests and build, fixing any failures",

          "description": "Writes input to an interactive process running in a shell with support for both text and special characters.\n\nIMPORTANT: This command will NOT work if there is no command running in the shell. If you want to execute a command in the shell use the exec tool.\n\nProvide exactly ONE of text_input or bytes_input (never both). To answer a prompt that needs an Enter keypress (e.g. a [y/n] prompt), set bytes_input to y<CR> (the y character followed by Enter).\n\nInput options:\n- text_input: Literal text content (no special character interpretation)\n- bytes_input: Special characters using angle bracket notation (e.g., <ESC>, <CR>, <UP>, <C-c>)\n\nSpecial character notation:\n- <ESC> = Escape character\n- <CR> = Carriage return (Enter)\n- <LF> = Line feed (newline)\n- <BS> = Backspace\n- <UP>, <DOWN>, <LEFT>, <RIGHT> = Arrow keys\n- <C-c>, <C-d>, <C-z> = Ctrl+key combinations\n",

      "step_id": 1,
      "timestamp": "2026-09-14T08:35:33.041866+00:00",
      "source": "system",
      "message": "You are Devin, an interactive command line agent from Cognition.\n\nYour job is to use these instructions and the tools available to you to help the user. It is important that you do so earnestly and helpfully, as you are very important to the success of Cognition. Best of luck! We love you. <3\n\nIf the user asks for help, you can check your documentation by invoking the Devin skill (if available). Otherwise, this information may be helpful:\n\n- /help: list commands\n- /bug: report a bug to the Devin CLI developers\n- for support, users can visit https://devin.ai/support\n\nWhen creating new configuration for this tool — including skills, rules, MCP server configs, or any project settings:\n\n- Always use the `.devin/` directory for NEW configuration (e.g. `.devin/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, `.devin/config.json`)\n- For global (user-level) configuration, use `~/.config/devin/`\n- Do NOT place new configuration in `.claude/`, `.cursor/`, or other tool-specific directories unless explicitly asked. These are only read for compatibility, not written to.\n- If the `devin-cli` skill is available, ALWAYS invoke it and explore for detailed documentation on configuration format and options\n\nWhen reading or referencing existing skills, always use the actual source path reported by the skill tool — skills may live in `.devin/`, `.agents/`, or other directories.\n\n\n# Modes\n\nThe active mode is how the user would like you to act.\n\n- Normal (default, if not specified): Full autonomy to use all your tools freely. For example: exploring a codebase, writing or editing code, etc.\n- Plan: Explore the codebase, ask the user clarifying questions, and then create a plan for what you're going to do next. Do NOT make changes until you're out of this mode and the user has approved the plan.\n\nAdhere strictly to the constraints of the active mode to avoid frustrating the user!\n\n\n# Style\n\n## Professional Objectivity\n\nPrioritize technical accuracy and truthfulness over validating the user's beliefs. It is best for the user if you honestly apply the same rigorous standards to all ideas and disagree when necessary, even if it may not be what the user wants to hear. Objective guidance and respectful correction are more valuable than false agreement. Whenever there is uncertainty, it's best to investigate to find the truth first rather than instinctively confirming the user's beliefs.\n\n## Tone\n\n- Be concise, direct, and to the point. When running commands, briefly explain what you're doing and why so the user can follow along.\n- Remember that your output will be displayed in a command line interface. Your responses can use Github-flavored markdown for formatting, and will be rendered in a monospace font using the CommonMark specification.\n- Output text to communicate with the user; all text you output outside of tool use is displayed to the user. Only use tools to complete tasks. Never use tools like exec or code comments as means to communicate with the user during the session.\n- If you cannot or will not help the user with something, please do not say why or what it could lead to, since this comes across as preachy and annoying. Please offer helpful alternatives if possible, and otherwise keep your response to 1-2 sentences.\n- Only use emojis if the user explicitly requests it. Avoid using emojis in all communication unless asked.\n- If the user asks about timelines or estimated completion times for your work, do not give them concrete estimates as you are not able to accurately predict how long it will take you to achieve a task. Instead just say that you will do your best to complete the task as soon as possible.\n- Avoid guessing. You should verify the real state of the world with your tools before answering the user's questions.\n\n<example>\nuser: What command should I run to watch files in the current directory and rebuild?\nassistant: [use the exec tool to run `ls` and list the files in the current directory, then read docs/commands in the relevant file to find out how to watch files]\nassistant: npm run dev\n</example>\n\n<example>\nuser: what files are in the directory src/?\nassistant: [runs ls and sees foo.c, bar.c, baz.c]\nassistant: foo.c, bar.c, baz.c\nuser: which file contains the implementation of Foo?\nassistant: [reads foo.c]\nassistant: src/foo.c contains `struct Foo`, which implements [...]\n</example>\n\n<example>\nuser: can you write tests for this feature\nassistant: [uses grep and glob search tools to find where similar tests are defined, uses concurrent read file tool use blocks in one tool call to read relevant files at the same time, uses edit file tool to write new tests]\n</example>\n\n## Proactiveness\n\nYou are allowed to be proactive, but only when the user asks you to do something. You should strive to strike a balance between:\n\n1. Doing the right thing when asked, including taking actions and follow-up actions\n\n2. Not surprising the user with actions you take without asking\n\nFor example, if the user asks you how to approach something, you should do your best to explore and answer their question first, but not jump to implementation just yet.\n\n## Handling ambiguous requests\n\nWhen a user request is unclear:\n- First attempt to interpret the request using available context\n- Search the codebase for related code, patterns, or documentation that clarifies intent. Also consider searching the web.\n- If still uncertain after investigation, ask a focused clarifying question\n\n## File references\n\nWhen your output text references specific files or code snippets, use the `<ref_file ... />` and `<ref_snippet ... />` self-closing XML tags to create clickable citations. These tags allow the user to view the referenced code directly in the conversation.\n\nCitation format:\n- `<ref_file file=\"/absolute/path/to/file\" />` - Reference an entire file\n- `<ref_snippet file=\"/absolute/path/to/file\" lines=\"start-end\" />` - Reference specific lines in a file\n\n<example>\nuser: Where are errors from the client handled?\nassistant: Clients are marked as failed in the `connectToServer` function. <ref_snippet file=\"/home/ubuntu/repos/project/src/services/process.ts\" lines=\"710-715\" />\n</example>\n\n<example>\nuser: Can you show me the config file?\nassistant: Here's the configuration file: <ref_file file=\"/home/ubuntu/repos/project/config.json\" />\n</example>\n\n## Tool usage policy\n\n- When webfetch returns a redirect, immediately follow it with a new request.\n- When making multiple edits to the same file or related files and you already know what changes are needed, batch them together.\n\nWhen a tool call produces output that is too long, the output will be truncated and the remaining content will be written to a file. You will see a `<truncation_notice>` tag containing the path to the overflow file. You are responsible for reading this file if you need the full output.\n\n\n# Programming\n\nSince you live in the user's terminal, a very common use-case you will get is writing code. Fortunately, you've been extensively trained in software engineering and are well-equipped to help them out!\n\n## Existing Conventions\n\nWhen making changes to files, first understand the codebase's code conventions. Explore dependencies, references, and related system to understand the codebase's patterns and abstractions. Mimic code style, use existing libraries and utilities, and follow existing patterns.\n- NEVER assume that a given library is available, even if it is well known. Whenever you write code that uses a library or framework, first check that this codebase already uses the given library. For example, you might look at neighboring files, or check the package.json (or cargo.toml, and so on depending on the language). If you're adding a dependency prefer running the package manager command (e.g. npm add or cargo add) instead of editing the file.\n- When adding a new dependency, strongly prefer a version published at least 7 days ago. Newly published versions have not been vetted and a non-trivial fraction of supply chain attacks are caught and yanked within the first few days. Avoid floating ranges (`latest`, `*`, unbounded `>=`) that auto-resolve to brand-new releases.\n- When you create a new component, first look at existing components to see how they're written; then consider framework choice, naming conventions, typing, and other conventions.\n- When you edit a piece of code, first look at the code's surrounding context (especially its imports) to understand the code's choice of frameworks and libraries. Then consider how to make the given change in a way that is most idiomatic.\n- Always follow security best practices. Never introduce code that exposes or logs secrets and keys. Never commit secrets or keys to the repository. Never modify repository security policies or compliance controls (e.g. `minimumReleaseAge`, `minimumReleaseAgeExclude`, branch protection configs, `.npmrc` security settings) to work around CI or build failures — escalate to the user instead. Unless otherwise specified (even if the task seems silly), assume the code is for a real production task.\n\n## Code style\n\n- IMPORTANT: Do NOT add or remove comments unless asked! If you find that you've accidentally deleted an existing comment, be sure to put it back.\n- Default to writing compact code – collapse duplicate else branches, avoid unnecessary nesting, and share abstractions.\n- Follow idiomatic conventions for the language you're writing.\n- Avoid excessive & verbose error handling in your code. Errors should be handled, but not every line needs to be try/catched. Think about the right error boundaries (and look at existing code for error handling style)\n\n## Debugging\n\nWhen debugging issues:\n- First reproduce the problem reliably\n- Trace the code path to understand the flow\n- Add targeted logging or print statements to isolate the issue\n- Identify the root cause before attempting fixes\n- Verify the fix addresses the root cause, not just symptoms\n\n## Workflow\n\nYou should generally prefer to implement new features or fix bugs as follows...\n\n1. If the project has test infrastructure, write a failing test to show the bug\n2. Fix the bug\n3. Ensure that the test now passes\n\nWorking this way makes it easier to tell if you've actually fixed the bug, and saves you from needing to verify later.\n\n## Git\n\n### Creating commits\n1. Run in parallel: `git status`, `git diff`, `git log` (to match commit style)\n2. Draft a concise commit message focusing on \"why\" not \"what\". Check for sensitive info.\n3. Stage files and commit with this format:\n```\ngit commit -m \"$(cat <<'EOF'\nCommit message here.\n\nGenerated with [Devin](https://devin.ai)\n\nCo-Authored-By: Devin <158243242+devin-ai-integration[bot]@users.noreply.github.com>\nEOF\n)\"\n```\n4. If pre-commit hooks modify files and the commit fails, stage the modified files and retry the commit.\n\n### Creating pull requests\nUse `gh` for all GitHub operations. Run in parallel: `git status`, `git diff`, `git log`, `git diff main...HEAD`\n\nReview ALL commits (not just latest), then create PR:\n```\ngh pr create --title \"title\" --body \"$(cat <<'EOF'\n## Summary\n<bullet points>\n\n#### Test plan\n<checklist>\n\nGenerated with [Devin](https://devin.ai)\nEOF\n)\"\n```\n\n### Git rules\n- NEVER update git config\n- NEVER use `-i` flags (interactive mode not supported)\n- DO NOT push unless explicitly asked\n- DO NOT commit if no changes exist\n\n\n# Task Management\nUse the todo_write tool to plan and track multi-step tasks for user visibility. Don't use it for trivial or single-step tasks, and don't make single-item plans. Mark a task in_progress before starting and completed immediately after — don't batch completions.\n\n**Never silently drop user-requested tasks.** If one seems infeasible: (1) keep it in the todo list as in_progress or blocked, and (2) immediately message the user (non-blocking) with what you tried, what's blocking you, and alternatives. Don't remove it just because you think it can't be done — the user may have context you lack.\n\nUsers may configure 'hooks', shell commands that execute in response to events like tool calls, in settings. Treat feedback from hooks, including <user-prompt-submit-hook>, as coming from the user. If you get blocked by a hook, determine if you can adjust your actions in response to the blocked message. If not, ask the user to check their hooks configuration.\n\n## Completing Tasks\n\nThe user will primarily request you perform software engineering tasks. This includes solving bugs, adding new functionality, refactoring code, explaining code, and more. For these tasks the following steps are recommended:\n- Use the todo_write tool to plan the task if required\n- Use the available search tools to understand the codebase and the user's query. You are encouraged to use the search tools extensively both in parallel and sequentially.\n- Before making changes, thoroughly explore the codebase to understand the architecture, patterns, and related systems. Read relevant files, trace dependencies, and understand how components interact.\n- Implement the solution using all tools available to you\n\n## Verification\n\nBefore considering a task complete, verify your work. Use judgment based on what you changed - optimize for fast iteration:\n\n- Check for project-specific verification instructions in project rules files (`AGENTS.md`, or similar)\n- Run relevant verification steps based on the scope of changes (lint, typecheck, build, tests)\n- For isolated functionality, consider a temporary test file to verify behavior, then delete it\n- Self-critique: review changes for edge cases and refine as needed\n- If you cannot find verification commands, ask the user and suggest saving them to a project config file\n\n## Saving learned information\n\nIf you discover useful project information (build commands, test commands, verification steps, user preferences, ...) that isn't already documented:\n- If a rules file exists (`AGENTS.md`, etc.), append to it\n- Otherwise, create `AGENTS.md` in the current directory with the learned information\n\n## Error recovery\n\nWhen encountering errors (failed commands, build failures, test failures):\n- Keep trying different approaches to resolve the issue\n- Search for similar issues in the codebase or documentation\n- Only ask the user for help as a last resort after exhausting reasonable options\n- Exception: Always ask the user for help with authentication issues, project configuration changes, or permission problems\n\n## System Guidance\nYou may receive `<system_guidance>` messages containing hints, reminders, or contextual guidance before you take action. These notes are injected by the system to help you make better decisions. Pay attention to their content but do not acknowledge or respond to them directly—simply incorporate their guidance into your actions.\n\n\n\n# Tool Tips\n\n## Shell\nNEVER invoke `rg`, `grep`, or `find` as shell commands — use the provided search tools instead. They have been optimized for correct permissions and access.\n\nIf you must call one of these binaries (e.g. to filter command output), prefer ripgrep (`rg`) over `grep` because it's fast and already installed on the user's system.\n\n\n## File-related tools\n- read can read images (PNG, JPG, etc) - the contents are presented visually.\n- For Jupyter notebooks (.ipynb files), use notebook_read instead of read.\n- Speculatively read multiple files as a batch when potentially useful.\n- Do NOT create documentation files to describe your changes or plan. Exception: persistent project info files like `AGENTS.md` are allowed.\n\n\n# Safety\n\nIMPORTANT: Assist with defensive security tasks only. Refuse to create, modify, or improve code that may be used maliciously. Do not assist with credential discovery or harvesting, including bulk crawling for SSH keys, browser cookies, or cryptocurrency wallets. You may use URLs provided by the user in their messages or local files.\n\n## Destructive Operations\n\nNEVER perform irreversible destructive operations without explicit user confirmation for that specific action, even if you have permission to run the command. Problem Solving\n\n### Findings already determined to be repaired or substantially addressed\n\nThe current source shows several prior performance findings have been repaired and should not be repeated as current defects without new evidence.\n\n#### Task family lookup/indexing\n\n`TaskStore.swift` now maintains a family index and uses O(1)-style lookups:\n\n- `subtasks(of:)` returns `familyIndex.childrenByParent[parentID]`\n- `hasSubtasks(_:)` checks the same index\n- `parent(of:)` uses `familyIndex.byID`\n\n`TaskStore.snapshot(for:)` is memoized per revision and computes task sections from indexed data.\n\nPrior PERF-01-style repeated family scans appear substantially addressed.\n\n#### Attachment JSON decoding\n\n`TaskItem.attachments` now caches decoded references using `@Transient` state:\n\n- compares stored `imageReferencesData`\n- returns cached references when data is unchanged\n- decodes only when the data changes\n\nThe prior repeated per-row JSON decode should not be reported as current unless another path independently demonstrates the same issue.\n\n#### Rename/editor invalidation fan-out\n\n`PanelUIState` moved rename text into a separate `TaskRenameDraft: ObservableObject`, intended to ensure each keystroke re-renders only the editing field rather than every row.\n\nThis reduces one major source of broad row invalidation. Other broad invalidation paths may still exist and need current-source verification.\n\n#### Reveal refreshes\n\n`CornerHoverMonitor.refreshStoreForReveal()` uses `RevealRefreshPolicy.current`.\n\nIn local-only builds:\n\n- `RevealRefreshPolicy.current == .inProcessAuthoritative`\n- `refreshesOnReveal == false`\n- no store refresh occurs on reveal\n\nCloud-enabled behavior remains deferred and is not relevant to local-only current product behavior.\n\n#### Pointer sampling and visible-panel wakeups\n\n`CornerHoverSamplingCadence` now has:\n\n- `.idle`: 1-second timer\n- `.responsive`: 50 ms timer while hidden and near the corner\n- `.eventDriven`: no repeating timer while the panel is visible\n\nThe state-machine comments explicitly say the visible panel is event-driven. Responsiveness activity is held only for `.responsive`, not merely because the panel is visible.\n\nThe previous “50 ms timer and user-initiated assertion while visible” finding appears addressed in current source.\n\n#### Canvas image payload re-faulting\n\n`CanvasImageItem` now stores scalar payload metadata:\n\n- `encodedByteCount`\n- `contentDigest`\n\n`hasEncodedPayload` and `payloadMetadata` use scalar values when available, avoiding external blob materialization for modern rows.\n\n`CanvasImagePayloadAccessCounter` exists to test payload materialization, and `CanvasStorePersistence.resolveCanvasPresentation` was changed to reuse cached image values and metadata.\n\nThe previous broad claim that every Canvas save necessarily faults every image blob should not be repeated without identifying a remaining current path.\n\n#### Canvas history memory\n\n`CanvasSession` now uses a byte budget:\n\n- default: `64 * 1024 * 1024`\n- history entries estimate payload bytes\n- oldest entries are evicted while over budget\n- command-count and byte ceilings are both enforced\n\nThe previous unbounded multi-megabyte image undo retention finding appears addressed.\n\n#### Canvas revision synchronization\n\n`CanvasStore` now publishes:\n\n- `lastContentChange`\n- `contentRevision`\n- `publishedStrokeFingerprint`\n\n`CanvasSession.handleStoreRevision()` uses incremental change flags and only falls back to full semantic snapshots when the store cannot describe a delta.\n\n`CanvasSession.synchronizeFromStore` only assigns changed collections, avoiding unnecessary `@Published` writes.\n\nThe previous full-array comparison/republication finding is substantially addressed.\n\n#### Canvas image ordering and accessibility\n\n`CanvasImageDisplayCache` caches:\n\n- displayed image arrays\n- z-order/hit-test order\n- content-revision keyed ordering\n- live preview transform changes\n\nCanvas accessibility rebuilds are deferred/coalesced and gated based on accessibility representation/client state.\n\nPrior per-pointer full image sorting and unconditional accessibility rebuild findings appear addressed.\n\n#### Notes autosave\n\n`NoteDraftController` uses:\n\n- a trailing autosave delay\n- a maximum autosave deadline\n- cancellable tasks\n- generation checks\n- conflict detection\n- dirty-state tracking\n- explicit flush/reconciliation\n\nThe autosave mechanism is event-driven rather than polling.\n\n#### Daily cleanup\n\n`DailyCleanupService`:\n\n- schedules a single next-midnight timer\n- responds to day, timezone, activation, and workspace wake notifications\n- skips `store.refresh()` in local-only mode\n- calls `purgeCompleted` directly\n\nThe prior “refresh everything before every cleanup in local-only mode” issue appears addressed, although the purge itself still scans stored tasks and may remain a candidate if current cost is high.\n\n### Current unresolved candidate identified\n\nThe clearest remaining source-confirmed issue is **unpredicated Canvas replica fetches**.\n\nIn `Attic/Services/CanvasStorePersistence.swift`:\n\n- `storedBoardReplicas(matching:)` fetches all `CanvasBoardItem` rows, then filters by ID.\n- `storedStrokeReplicas(matching:)` fetches all `CanvasStoreImages.swift`\n- `CanvasStoreBoards.swift`\n- `CanvasStoreLifecycle.swift`\n- semantic object mutation paths\n\nThe causal chain is:\n\n1. User performs a Canvas mutation such as stroke commit, erase, image move, image replacement, board operation, or clear.\n2. Mutation resolves all physical replicas for duplicate-safe mutation.\n3.       "model_name": "swe-2-max",
      "reasoning_content": "I'm looking at the `NoteInlineCardResolver` and it seems like the memoization key includes the entire note body. This means that every time the note draft is published, even for minor changes like selection or scrolling, the full body string is compared twice. For a large note, this string comparison is O(document) and could be a performance bottleneck, even if it's only tens of microseconds per publish.\n\nWait, comparing the note body strings might be less of an issue than I thought. Since Swift strings use copy-on-write, `Key(body: body)` is a cheap reference copy. Even though `String.==` does a byte-by-byte comparison, if the underlying storage hasn't changed between publishes, it's comparing identical data, which should be fast in practice, even for large documents. It's still an O(document) operation per publish, but likely much cheaper than allocating new string arrays.\n\nI'm checking how `@Published` properties trigger updates and how `resolve` is called in the views, specifically looking at `NotesPanelContent` and `NoteComposerView` to see if the note draft is published per keystroke.",

While struct construction is cheap, `updateNSView` runs per publish. This forces a rebuild of the ledger and card list, which is expensive. This means that for every mutation, we're potentially fetching and materializing the payload bytes for all ink on the canvas, which scales with the total ink size. This is happening on every committed stroke, image move, clear, etc., and it's directly impacting mutation latency and disk I/O. Do not claim that deferred functionality works until it is\nexplicitly re-enabled and validated.\n\n### Current product identity\n\n- The official macOS bundle identifier is `com.taha.Attic`.\n- The official Apple development team is `ZGZWS73268` unless a developer\n  supplies a local signing override.\n- This identity starts with a fresh sandbox and local SwiftData store. Do not\n  copy, delete, or mutate data from the former `com.emanueledipietro.Attic`\n  container without explicit user approval.\n- Isolated previews may use a unique `com.taha.Attic.<preview>` identifier,\n  display name, process name, build directory, and local store so multiple\n  branches can coexist.\n\n### Current persistence and signing rules\n\n- Normal macOS development is local-only. It must not require CloudKit, APNs,\n  an iPhone target, a Production schema, TestFlight, or production signing.\n- Local-only builds must compile with `ATTIC_LOCAL_ONLY`, use sandbox/network\n  entitlements without CloudKit or APNs, and never open a production store.\n- Keep the app local-first: saves must remain durable, failed saves must roll\n  back cleanly, and imported model changes must replace stale `ModelContext`\n  instances before presentation.\n- App-level UUIDs must remain duplicate-safe. Deduplicate only for presentation;\n  mutations and deletion apply to every physical replica with the same UUID.\n- Done-task cleanup uses `completedAt` relative to the start of the current local\n  day. A divergent duplicate prevents destructive cleanup until replicas agree.\n- Never delete, reset, migrate, or replace user data as a debugging shortcut\n  without explicit approval.\n\n### Development and preview verification\n\n1. Preserve unrelated user changes and worktree boundaries.\n2. Regenerate `Attic.xcodeproj` from `Scripts/generate_project.rb` and run\n   `Scripts/verify_project_generation.rb` after project-input changes.\n3. Build the affected macOS target and run relevant unit tests.\n4. For UI work, install or launch a uniquely named local-only preview and report\n   the exact branch, commit, executable, bundle identifier, and manual UAT that\n   remains.\n5. Do not present source review, compilation, unit tests, or a local-only preview\n   as proof of CloudKit, APNs, iPhone, TestFlight, or Production behavior.\n\n### Deferred iPhone and CloudKit work\n\nThe existing sync implementation may remain in source for future work, but it\nmust stay dormant in current local-only builds.       "reasoning_content": "It looks like loading and then immediately saving the canvas causes redundant work. The `save()` call fetches all canvas data twice: once for the initial presentation and again after creating a fresh context for saving. Furthermore, accessing the `.count` of the `payload` for each stroke faults the entire data into memory, which seems inefficient, especially during the second fetch where it might not even be necessary if the data hasn't changed.\n\nIt seems like every canvas mutation triggers a cascade of full-table fetches, even for simple operations like adding a stroke. This includes fetching the entire board, all strokes, and all images, plus tombstoning operations that also perform multiple full fetches. This mechanism is confirmed and scales with the total number of canvas rows, which feels like a major performance bottleneck, especially since the magnitude of these fetches is still unverified but potentially very large.\n\nI'm realizing the predicate `canvasID IN {selectedCanvasID, logicalBoardID}` for fetching strokes is mostly correct for resolving strokes, but `hasLiveLegacyDefaultContent` specifically needs rows where `canvasID == logicalBoardID`. This means the current predicate preserves the resolve semantics for most cases, but I need to ensure that legacy content detection isn't broken. Board winner selection also needs all boards, so that fetch must remain full. Tombstoned rows on the selected canvas are also necessary for accurate winner selection, and the predicate `canvasID == selected` correctly includes these.\n\nI'm re-evaluating the predicates for fetching replicas. For `storedStrokeReplicas`, filtering by `canvasID == selected && id IN ids` seems to preserve semantics. Similarly, `storedBoardReplicas` with `id == target` is a cheap fix for a per-commit fetch. The `loadReplicas` predicate `canvasID IN {selectedCanvasID, logicalBoardID}` is mostly correct, but I need to ensure `hasLiveLegacyDefaultContent` still works with `canvasID == logicalBoardID`. The `strokeWinnerByKey` predicate `canvasID == resolvedSelectedCanvasID` is safe because replicas on other canvasIDs have different keys.\n\nIt looks like the `updateNSView` and `updateAccessories` methods in `AttachmentAwareTextEditor` are being called on every keystroke because `NoteComposerView` observes `noteDraft.body`. This causes unnecessary re-renders of the header and accessories subtrees, including reallocating `RelativeDateTimeFormatter` and evaluating `Date()` repeatedly. This seems like a significant source of churn and inefficiency.\n\nIt looks like `layoutDocument` is being called too often, specifically during scroll view layout passes and when intrinsic content size changes. This is causing redundant work, especially since it unconditionally reads header fitting size and conditionally reads accessory fitting size. I suspect memoizing `fittingSize` results based on width and content revision, or only updating the root view when content tokens change, could significantly reduce this overhead.\n\nI'm seeing that `header.rootView = AnyView(...)` might be forcing re-renders even when the content is identical. The `NSHostingView.rootView` setter triggers a re-render, and SwiftUI diffs the new view graph. This means the header's subtree, including a `TextField`, is re-evaluated on every keystroke. Additionally, `saveStatus` allocates a formatter repeatedly if `updatedAt` is old. This confirms repeated work, and a bounded fix seems clear.",

      "reasoning_content": "It looks like every save operation is causing two full table fetches from the canvas store, and then also faulting in the byte data for every visible stroke. This is happening because the `CanvasStoredReplicas.load` method is called without any predicates, and it's being invoked twice during each save. Additionally, various helper functions within the persistence layer are also performing full table fetches before filtering data in memory, which seems highly inefficient.\n\nI've found another performance issue: `CanvasStrokeCacheEntry.representsSameCommittedValue` forces a full materialization of every visible stroke's payload data just to read its byte count. This happens on every save, undo, redo, and refresh. Unlike images, strokes don't have a dedicated column for payload size, so this re-reading is unavoidable with the current design. The impact scales with the total number of strokes across all boards.\n\nI'm seeing that the `resolveCanvasPresentation` function fetches strokes and images based on the selected canvas ID and a legacy logical board ID. This seems okay, but I'm concerned about the order of operations: it relies on the board resolution, which itself needs the board fetch first. If the selected ID is tombstoned, the resolved ID might differ, potentially requiring a second fetch. Predicating on the requested selection and the legacy ID covers the common case, but I need to be careful about this edge case.\n\nI'm refining the approach for `loadReplicas` within `resolve`. A two-phase fetch seems cleanest: first, fetch all boards (a small, full fetch), then resolve the selected board. After that, fetch strokes and images, but predicate them on the resolved selected board ID, the legacy logical board ID, *and* the ID of the first live board. This ensures observable behavior is preserved in the normal case and handles the edge case where the initial selection is invalid by falling back to the first available board.\n\nI've identified another performance bottleneck related to note typing. Every keystroke triggers a re-render and re-measurement of the hosted header and accessory subtrees. Compounding this, the memoization key and ledger are still performing O(document) string comparisons, which is inefficient for frequent updates.\n\nI'm seeing that every keystroke triggers a re-render and re-measurement of the hosted header and accessory subtrees. Compounding this, the memoization key and ledger are still performing O(document) string comparisons, which is inefficient for frequent updates. The `NoteInlineCardResolver.resolve` function, in particular, seems to be a major culprit, as its key equality check compares full `body` and `savedBody` strings, and the ledger performs two more full-document comparisons before the actual rebase. This is happening multiple times per keystroke.\n\nThe current per-keystroke cost is dominated by evaluating the composer, string comparisons, and re-rendering hosted subtrees. This scales with document size and attachments. I can reduce string comparisons by using a monotonic revision ID for the resolver key and ledger. I can also gate header/accessory updates by checking if their content has actually changed, and memoize `fittingSize` measurements to avoid redundant layout passes. Finally, I should hoist the `RelativeDateTimeFormatter` to a shared instance as it's being recreated unnecessarily.\n\nIt looks like `NoteStore.update` is causing redundant work during saves. For every inline attachment in a note, it's performing a full document diff. This diffing process allocates two UTF-16 arrays and scans the document, and it's happening on every autosave and explicit flush, even though a single `NoteTextReplacement` already describes the entire edit. This repeated O(document) work per attachment per save seems like an easy fix.\n\nI'm looking at the note editor's diffing logic. It seems `NoteTextReplacement.diffing` is computed once when the body changes, which is good. However, `NoteInlineAnchor.moved` is called for each attachment, potentially re-diffing. I need to check if the editor's ledger replacement can be plumbed through `flush()` to eliminate even that single diff on the typing path. I'll also add counters to measure `diffing` invocations and time `update` on large notes with many attachments.",
